To find the slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   - First point: \((x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)\)
   - Second point: \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

2. **Apply the slope formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]

3. **Substitute the values into the formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{12}{-8}
   \]

4. **Calculate the slope:**
   \[
   m = -\frac{12}{8} = -\frac{3}{2} = -1.5
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The slope of the line is \(-\frac{3}{2}\) or \(-1.5\).

\[
m = \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}
\]

To find the slope of the second line when two lines have an angle of \(\frac{\pi}{4}\) between them and one of the lines has a slope of \(\frac{1}{4}\), we can use the formula for the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

Given:
- \(\theta = \frac{\pi}{4}\)
- \(m_1 = \frac{1}{4}\)

We know that \(\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 1\). Plugging in the values:

\[
1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

This results in two possible equations due to the absolute value:

1. \( \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = 1 \)
2. \( \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = -1 \)

Solving the first equation:
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
0.75 m_2 = 1.25 \\
m_2 = \frac{5}{3} \approx 1.\overline{6}
\]

Solving the second equation:
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
1.5 m_2 = -0.75 \\
m_2 = -0.5
\]

Therefore, the possible slopes of the second line are:

\[
\boxed{\left\{ \text{Final Answer}: \left\{ \text{'Numeric Answer'}: 1.\overline{6} \text{, } -0.5 \right\} \right\}}
\]

To determine the value of \( x \) for the point \((x,\ 26)\) lying on the line passing through the points \((10,\ 14)\) and \((x,\ 26)\), given that this line is parallel to the line passing through the points \((0,\ 8)\) and \((6,\ 10)\), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope of the First Line**

The first line passes through the points \((0,\ 8)\) and \((6,\ 10)\).

\[
\text{Slope (m)} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Second Line**

Since the lines are parallel, their slopes must be equal. Therefore, the slope of the second line is also \( \frac{1}{3} \).

**Step 3: Use the Slope to Find \( x \)**

The second line passes through the points \((10,\ 14)\) and \((x,\ 26)\). Using the slope formula:

\[
\text{Slope (m)} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

Set the slope equal to \( \frac{1}{3} \):

\[
\frac{12}{x - 10} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \( x \)**

Cross-multiplied:

\[
12 \times 3 = (x - 10) \times 1 \\
36 = x - 10 \\
x = 36 + 10 \\
x = 46
\]

**Final Answer**

The value of \( x \) is \(\boxed{46}\).

Shoelace formula:

Where point n+1 is point 1.

To determine the distance from point \( C(7, -3) \) to the line passing through points \( A(-6, 7) \) and \( B(2, 9) \), we first find the equation of the line \( AB \).

1. Calculate the slope \( m \) of line \( AB \):
   \[
   m = \frac{9 - 7}{2 - (-6)} = \frac{2}{8} = \frac{1}{4}
   \]

2. Use point-slope form to find the equation of line \( AB \) using point \( A(-6, 7) \):
   \[
   y - 7 = \frac{1}{4}(x + 6)
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{3}{2} + 7 \implies y = \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{17}{2}
   \]

3. Convert the equation to standard form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \):
   \[
   x - 4y + 34 = 0
   \]
   Here, \( A = 1 \), \( B = -4 \), and \( C = 34 \).

4. Calculate the distance from point \( (7, -3) \) to this line using the formula for distance from a point to a line:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} = \frac{|1 \cdot 7 + (-4) \cdot (-3) + 34|}{\sqrt{1^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{|53|}{\sqrt{17}} = \frac{53}{\sqrt{17}}
   \]
   Rationalizing the denominator, we get:
   \[
   \frac{53}{\sqrt{17}} = \frac{53 \sqrt{17}}{17}
   \]

To check if points \( A(-6, 7) \), \( B(2, 9) \), \( C(7, -3) \), and \( D(-2, 0) \) form a rectangle:

1. Calculate midpoints of diagonals:
   - Midpoint of \( AC \): 
     \[
     \left( \frac{-6 + 7}{2}, \frac{7 - 3}{2} \right) = \left( \frac{1}{2}, 2 \right)
     \]
   - Midpoint of \( BD \):
     \[
     \left( \frac{2 + (-2)}{2}, \frac{9 + 0}{2} \right) = (0, 4.5)
     \]
   Since the midpoints are different, the points do not form a rectangle.

Thus, the distance from point \( C \) to line \( AB \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{53\sqrt{17}}{17}}\) units, and the points do not form a rectangle.

To find a point on the **x-axis** that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Problem
We need to find a point \((x, 0)\) on the x-axis such that its distance from both \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\) is equal.

### Step 2: Apply the Distance Formula
The distance between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Set up equations for the distances from \((x, 0)\) to both points and set them equal since they must be equidistant.

### Step 3: Set Up the Equations
1. Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((9, 8)\):
\[
\sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64}
\]

2. Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((5, 6)\):
\[
\sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
\]

Set these distances equal:
\[
\sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
\]

### Step 4: Square Both Sides to Eliminate the Square Roots
\[
(9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
\]

### Step 5: Expand and Simplify
Expand both squared terms:
\[
81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
145 - 18x = 61 - 10x
\]

Subtract \(61\) from both sides and add \(18x\) to both sides:
\[
-18x + 10x = 61 - 145
\]
\[
-8x = -84
\]

Divide both sides by \(-8\):
\[

\]

### Step 7: Determine the Point on the x-axis
The point is \((10.5, 0)\).

First, I need to determine the slopes of two lines where one slope is twice the other. Let's denote the smaller slope as \( m \). Therefore, the other slope will be \( 2m \).

The tangent of the angle between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) can be calculated using the formula:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]
Given that one slope is twice the other, we substitute \( m_1 = m \) and \( m_2 = 2m \).

Plugging these values into the formula:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (m)(2m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
\]
We are given that \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \). Therefore, we can set up the equation:
\[
\left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Since both numerator and denominator are positive (assuming \( m > 0 \)), we can remove the absolute value:
\[
\frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Cross-multiplying to eliminate the fractions:
\[
3m = 1 + 2m^2
\]
Rearranging the terms to form a quadratic equation:
\[
2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
\]
To solve for \( m \), we use the quadratic formula:
\[
m = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 8}}{4} = \frac{3 \pm 1}{4}
\]
This yields two solutions:
\[
m = \frac{4}{4} = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad m = \frac{2}{4} = 0.5
\]
Therefore, the smaller slope \( m \) is either \( 1 \) or \( 0.5 \). Consequently, the other slopes are:
- If \( m = 1 \), then \( m_2 = 2(1) = 2 \)
- If \( m = 0.5 \), then \( m_2 = 2(0.5) = 1 \)

Both sets of solutions satisfy the given conditions.
</think>

To solve the problem, let's follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Slopes:**
   - Let the smaller slope be \( m \).
   - The other slope is twice that, so it is \( 2m \).

2. **Use the Tangent Formula for the Angle Between Two Lines:**
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
   \]
   Substituting \( m_1 = m \) and \( m_2 = 2m \):
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (m)(2m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
   \]
   
3. **Set Up the Equation:**
   Given \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \):
   \[
   \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
   
4. **Solve for \( m \):**
   Assuming \( m > 0 \) (since slope can be negative, but we're looking for the smaller magnitude):
   \[
   \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
   
5. **Cross-Multiply to Eliminate Fractions:**
   \[
   3m = 1 + 2m^2
   \]
   
6. **Rearrange the Equation:**
   \[
   2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
   \]
   
7. **Solve the Quadratic Equation:**
   Using the quadratic formula \( m = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \) where \( a = 2 \), \( b = -3 \), and \( c = 1 \):
   \[
   m = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 8}}{4} = \frac{3 \pm 1}{4}
   \]
   
8. **Find the Solutions:**
   \[
   m = \frac{4}{4} = 1 \quad \text{or} \quad m = \frac{2}{4} = 0.5
   \]
   
9. **Determine Both Slopes for Each Case:**
   - If \( m = 1 \):
     - The other slope is \( 2m = 2(1) = 2 \)
   - If \( m = 0.5 \):
     - The other slope is \( 2m = 2(0.5) = 1 \)

**Final Answer:**
The slopes of the two lines are either \( 1 \) and \( 2 \), or \( 0.5 \) and \( 1 \).

To determine the equation of the line passing through the points \((-1, 4)\) and \((2, -3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope (m)
The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given values:
\[
m = \frac{-3 - 4}{2 - (-1)} = \frac{-7}{3} = -\frac{7}{3}
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form
The point-slope form of a line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using the first point \((-1, 4)\) and the slope \(m = -\frac{7}{3}\):
\[
y - 4 = -\frac{7}{3}(x - (-1)) \\
y - 4 = -\frac{7}{3}(x + 1)
\]

### Step 3: Convert to Slope-Intercept Form (Optional)
To express the equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)):
\[
y - 4 = -\frac{7}{3}x - \frac{7}{3}
\]
Add 4 to both sides:
\[
y = -\frac{7}{3}x - \frac{7}{3} + 4 \\
y = -\frac{7}{3}x + \frac{5}{3}
\]

### Step 4: Final Equation in Standard Form
To present the equation in standard form (\( Ax + By = C \)):
Start from the point-slope form:
\[
y - 4 = -\frac{7}{3}(x + 1)
\]
Multiply both sides by 3 to eliminate fractions:
\[
3(y - 4) = -7(x + 1) \\
3y - 12 = -7x - 7
\]
Rearrange the terms:
\[
7x + 3y = 5
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line in standard form is:
\[
\boxed{7x + 3y = 5}
\]

1. \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 6 \), which intersects the y-axis at (0, 6)
2. \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \), which intersects the y-axis at (0, -2)

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Equation of the Original Line:**
   The original line passes through the point (0,2) and has a slope based on an angle of 150 degrees from the positive x-axis.
   
   - Compute the slope \( m \):
     \[
     m = \tan(150^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
     \]
     
   - Use the point-slope form:
     \[
     y - 2 = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}(x - 0)
     \]
     Simplifying, we get \( y = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}x + 2 \).
   
   However, considering the angle with respect to direction might have led to a slope of \( m = -\sqrt{3} \), resulting in:
   \[
   y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
   \]
   
2. **Determine Parallel Lines at Distance 2:**
   To find lines parallel to the original line and at a distance of 2 units, use the formula for distance between parallel lines \( Ax + By + C_1 = 0 \) and \( Ax + By + C_2 = 0 \):
   
   - The standard form for both equations is:
     Original: \( \sqrt{3}x + y - 2 = 0 \)
     
   - New line(s): \( \sqrt{3}x + y - k = 0 \)
   
   - Apply the distance formula (distance d between two parallel lines):
     \[
     d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \\
     2 = \frac{|-2 - (-k)|}{\sqrt{ (\sqrt{3})^2 + 1 }} \\
     2 = \frac{|k - 2|}{2} \\
     |k - 2| = 4 \\
     k = 6 \text{ or } k = -2
     \]
   
   Thus, the parallel lines are:
   \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 6 \) and \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \).

3. **Find Y-Intercepts:**
   For each line, when x=0, find y:

   - First Line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}(0) + 6 = 6 \)
   
   - Second Line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}(0) -2 = -2 \)

**Final Answer:**
- Original line equation: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \)
- Parallel lines at distance 2:
  - Equation: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 6 \), Y-intercept: (0, 6)
  - Equation: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \), Y-intercept: (0, -2)

First, I need to determine the equation of the line that intersects both axes at points with equal coordinates. Let's assume these intersection points are \((a, 0)\) on the x-axis and \((0, a)\) on the y-axis since their abscissa and ordinate are equal.

Next, I'll use the two-point form to find the equation of the line passing through these points: \((a, 0)\) and \((0, a)\). The slope \(m\) can be calculated as:
\[
m = \frac{a - 0}{0 - a} = -1
\]
With the slope known, I'll use the point-slope form to derive the equation of the line. Using the y-intercept at \((0, a)\):
\[
y = mx + b \implies y = -x + a
\]
So, the equation of the line is \(x + y = a\).

Now, I need to find the value of \(a\) such that the line passes through the point (4, 5). Substituting these coordinates into the equation:
\[
4 + 5 = a \implies a = 9
\]
With \(a\) determined, the final equation of the line is:
\[
x + y = 9
\]
</think>

To determine the equation of the line that intersects both axes at points with equal coordinates and passes through the point (4, 5), follow these steps:

1. **Identify Intersection Points:**
   - Let the x-intercept be \((a, 0)\).
   - Since the abscissa and ordinate are equal on both axes, the y-intercept is also \((0, a)\).

2. **Find the Slope of the Line:**
   \[
   m = \frac{a - 0}{0 - a} = -1
   \]

3. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**
   Using the y-intercept \((0, a)\):
   \[
   y = mx + b \implies y = -x + a
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the Given Point (4, 5) into the Equation:**
   \[
   5 = -4 + a \implies a = 9
   \]

5. **Final Equation of the Line:**
   Substitute \(a = 9\) back into the equation:
   \[
   x + y = 9
   \]

**Final Answer:**

First, I need to determine the slope of the line passing through the two points (1, 0) and (2, 3). The formula for calculating the slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points into the formula:

\[
m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3
\]

Now that I have the slope, I can use the point-slope form of a linear equation to find the equation of the line. The point-slope form is:

\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]

Using one of the points, for example (1, 0), and substituting \( m = 3 \):

\[
y - 0 = 3(x - 1)
\]

Simplifying this equation gives:

\[
y = 3x - 3
\]

Therefore, the equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:

\[
y = 3x - 3
\]
</think>

To determine the equation of the line passing through the points (1, 0) and (2, 3), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Line
The point-slope form of a linear equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using the point (1, 0) and substituting \( m = 3 \):
\[
y - 0 = 3(x - 1)
\]
Simplifying this equation gives:
\[
y = 3x - 3
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:

Okay, so the first thing I need to do is calculate the slope (m). The formula for slope between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is given by m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). Let me plug in the values from the points they've given: (4,7) as (x1,y1) and (-5,8) as (x2,y2).

So substituting these into the formula, I get m = (8 - 7)/(-5 - 4). That simplifies to m = (1)/(-9), which means m is -1/9. Hmm, that's a negative slope, so the line is decreasing as x increases.

Now that I have the slope, the next step is to find the y-intercept (b). For this, I can use one of the given points and plug it into the equation y = mx + b along with the slope we just found. Let me choose the first point (4,7) for substitution.

Plugging in x=4, y=7, and m=-1/9, the equation becomes 7 = (-1/9)*4 + b. Calculating that, I get 7 = -4/9 + b. To solve for b, I need to add 4/9 to both sides of the equation. So, b = 7 + 4/9.

Wait a second, adding 7 and 4/9 isn't straightforward because they're not like terms. Let me convert 7 into ninths to make it easier. Since 1 is equal to 9/9, then 7 is 63/9. So adding 4/9 gives b = 63/9 + 4/9 = 67/9.

Now that I have both the slope and the y-intercept, I can write the equation of the line by substituting these values back into the slope-intercept form. Therefore, the equation is y = (-1/9)x + 67/9.

Let me just double-check my calculations to make sure I didn't make any mistakes. Starting with the slope: (8-7)/(-5-4) = 1/-9 = -1/9. That seems correct. Then using point (4,7): 7 = (-1/9)*4 + b → 7 = -4/9 + b → b=7+4/9=63/9+4/9=67/9. Yep, that checks out.

So the final equation of the line passing through the points (4,7) and (-5,8) is y = (-1/9)x + 67/9.

The equation of the line can be found using the two given points. Here's how:

1. **Calculate the slope (m):**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]

2. **Use one point to find the y-intercept (b):**
   Using point (4, 7):
   \[
   7 = -\frac{1}{9}(4) + b \\
   7 = -\frac{4}{9} + b \\
   b = 7 + \frac{4}{9} = \frac{63}{9} + \frac{4}{9} = \frac{67}{9}
   \]

3. **Write the equation of the line:**
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{9}x + \frac{67}{9}
   \]

So to match the general form, it's 5x + (-6)y + (-28) = 0, so a=5, b=-6, and c=-28.

Now, the point in question is (5, -7). Let me denote this as (x₀, y₀), which means x₀ is 5 and y₀ is -7. 

The distance formula from a point to a line is given by |ax₀ + by₀ + c| divided by the square root of (a² + b²). So putting it all together, the numerator would be |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) + (-28)|, and the denominator will be sqrt(5² + (-6)²).

Let me compute the numerator step by step. First, 5 multiplied by 5 is 25. Then, -6 multiplied by -7 gives me positive 42 because a negative times a negative is a positive. Adding those two together: 25 + 42 equals 67. Now, I add c which is -28 to this sum. So that's 67 minus 28, which equals 39.

Since the numerator in the distance formula is an absolute value, |39| is just 39 because 39 is already positive.

Now moving on to the denominator: sqrt(5² + (-6)²). Let me compute each term inside the square root. 5 squared is 25, and (-6) squared is also 36 (since squaring a negative makes it positive). Adding those together gives me 25 + 36 = 61.

So now I have to take the square root of 61 for the denominator. Hmm, sqrt(61)... I don't think that simplifies further since 61 is a prime number and its square root isn't an integer. So it remains as sqrt(61).

Putting it all together, the distance is 39 divided by sqrt(61). But wait, usually, we rationalize the denominator when possible to make the expression cleaner. To do that, I can multiply both the numerator and the denominator by sqrt(61), which would give me (39*sqrt(61)) / (sqrt(61)*sqrt(61)). Since sqrt(61) times sqrt(61) is just 61, this simplifies to (39*sqrt(61))/61.

So the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x -6y -28 =0 is 39 divided by sqrt(61), or equivalently, (39*sqrt(61))/61. But I think leaving it as 39/sqrt(61) might be acceptable depending on what's required in terms of form.

To find the distance between the two parallel lines \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here:
- For the first line, \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\), we have \(A = 3\), \(B = -4\), and \(C_1 = 12\).
- For the second line, \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), we have \(A = 3\), \(B = -4\), and \(C_2 = 7\).

Plugging these values into the formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

Thus, the distance between the two lines is 1.

Now, to find the altitude from point A to line BC, I'll use the formula for the distance from a point (x₀, y₀) to the line ax + by + c = 0: |ax₀ + by₀ + c| / √(a² + b²). **Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Assume the slope (M):**
   - Assume that "kharar" implies a 45-degree angle, so M is 1.

2. **Use the point-slope formula:**
   Plug in X=2 and Y=4 into \( Y = MX + C \):
   \( 4 = 1*2 + C \)
   
3. **Solve for C:**
   Subtract 2 from both sides:
   \( C = 4 - 2 = 2 \)

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We need to find the foot of the perpendicular from the point (1, 5) onto the line given by the equation \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\). 

**Step 1: Understand the Given Information**
- **Point:** (1, 5)
- **Line Equation:** \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\)

**Step 2: Find the Slope of the Given Line**
The equation is in standard form. Let's convert it to slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to find the slope (m).

Starting with:
\[3x - 4y - 16 = 0\]
Subtract \(3x\) and add 16 to both sides:
\[-4y = -3x + 16\]
Divide by \(-4\):
\[y = \frac{3}{4}x - 4\]

So, the slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{3}{4}\).

**Step 3: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line**
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal. 

\[ m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{\frac{3}{4}} = -\frac{4}{3} \]

**Step 4: Write the Equation of the Perpendicular Line**
This perpendicular line passes through (1, 5). Using point-slope form:

\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]
Plugging in:
\[ y - 5 = -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1) \]

Simplify to slope-intercept form:
\[ y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3} + 5 \]
Convert 5 to thirds: \(5 = \frac{15}{3}\)
So,
\[ y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3} \]

**Step 5: Find the Intersection Point (Foot of Perpendicular)**
Set the two equations equal to each other:

\[\frac{3}{4}x - 4 = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}\]

Multiply both sides by 12 to eliminate denominators:
\[9x - 48 = -16x + 76\]

Combine like terms:
\[9x + 16x = 76 + 48\]
\[25x = 124\]
\[x = \frac{124}{25} = 4.96\]

**Step 6: Find y-coordinate**
Plug \(x\) back into one of the line equations, say \(y = \frac{3}{4}x - 4\):
\[ y = \frac{3}{4} \times \frac{124}{25} - 4 \]
Calculate:
\[ y = \frac{372}{100} - 4 = 3.72 - 4 = -0.28 \]

**Step 7: Final Answer**
The foot of the perpendicular is at:
\[\left( \frac{124}{25}, -\frac{7}{25} \right)\]
</think>

The foot of the perpendicular from the point (1, 5) onto the line \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\) can be found using the steps outlined above.

**Final Answer:**

{'Foot of Perpendicular': {\(x\): 124/25, \(y\): -7/25}}

To find the value of \( H \), we start by noting that the point \((H, 5)\) lies on both the line connecting the points \((H, 5)\) and \((6, 3)\) (which is the function \( f(x) \)) and also lies on the line given by the equation \( 7x - 9y = 19 \).

1. Substitute \( x = H \) into the equation \( 7x - 9y = 19 \):
   \[
   7H - 9y = 19
   \]
2. Since \( y = 5 \) at \( x = H \), we substitute \( y = 5 \) into the equation:
   \[
   7H - 9(5) = 19
   \]
3. Simplify the equation:
   \[
   7H - 45 = 19
   \]
4. Solve for \( H \):
   \[
   7H = 64
   \]
   \[
   H = \frac{64}{7}
   \]

Thus, the value of \( H \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{64}{7}}\).

cosθ = (v1 • v2) / (|v1||v2|)

So cosθ = sqrt(3)/2. Therefore, θ = arccos(sqrt(3)/2), which is 30 degrees. So that confirms it.

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations √3 x + y = 1 and x + √3 y = 1, we first determine their slopes. 

For the line √3 x + y = 1:
- Rearranging to slope-intercept form gives y = -√3 x + 1.
- The slope (m₁) is -√3.

For the line x + √3 y = 1:
- Rearranging similarly, we get y = (-1/√3)x + 1/√3.
- The slope (m₂) is -1/√3.

Using the formula for the angle θ between two lines with slopes m₁ and m₂:
\[ \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m₂ - m₁}{1 + m₁ \cdot m₂} \right| \]

Substituting the values:
\[ \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3})(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})} \right| = \left| \frac{\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| = \left| \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right| \]

Thus, θ = 30°.

Answer: The angle between the lines is 30 degrees.

To find a line parallel to the given line \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) that passes through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Understand Parallel Lines**:
   - Parallel lines have the same slope.
   - Therefore, their equations will have the same coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\).

2. **General Form of a Parallel Line**:
   - The given line is in the form \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\).
   - A parallel line will be in the form \(5x - 6y + c = 0\), where \(c\) is a constant.

3. **Find the Constant \(c\)**:
   - Substitute the coordinates of the point \((-4, 5)\) into the equation to find \(c\):
     \[
     5(-4) - 6(5) + c = 0
     \]
     \[
     -20 - 30 + c = 0
     \]
     \[
     -50 + c = 0
     \]
     \[
     c = 50
     \]

4. **Write the Final Equation**:
   - Substitute \(c = 50\) back into the equation:
     \[
     5x - 6y + 50 = 0
     \]

**Final Answer**:

To find the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line defined by the equation \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), follow these steps:

1. **Simplify the Line Equation**:
   
   Start by expanding and rearranging the given equation to its standard form.

   \[
   24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)
   \]
   
   Expand both sides:
   
   \[
   24x + 288 = 10y - 40
   \]
   
   Rearrange to standard form \(Ax + By + C = 0\):
   
   \[
   24x - 10y + 328 = 0
   \]

2. **Identify Coefficients**:
   
   From the standard form, identify the coefficients:
   
   \[
   A = 24,\quad B = -10,\quad C = 328
   \]

3. **Apply Distance Formula**:
   
   The distance \(D\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:
   
   \[
   D = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]
   
   Substitute the known values:
   
   \[
   D = \frac{|24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}}
   \]

4. **Calculate Numerator and Denominator**:
   
   - **Numerator**:
     
     \[
     |24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328| = |-48 - 20 + 328| = |260| = 260
     \]
   
   - **Denominator**:
     
     \[
     \sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26
     \]

5. **Compute the Distance**:
   
   \[
   D = \frac{260}{26} = 10
   \]

**Final Answer**:  
\boxed{\text{Distance is }10\text{ units}}

   \[

   \[

Given a system of equations \( S \) and \( S' \):

- System \( S \): \( y = -x + 6 \)
- System \( S' \): \( y = x + 4 \)

To find the intersection point, we solve these equations simultaneously:

1. Set the equations equal to each other:
   \[
   -x + 6 = x + 4
   \]
2. Bring variables to one side:
   \[
   -x - x = 4 - 6 \implies -2x = -2
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = 1
   \]
4. Substitute \( x = 1 \) back into one of the equations, for example \( y = x + 4 \):
   \[
   y = 1 + 4 = 5
   \]

Thus, the point of intersection is \((1, 5)\).

\[
\boxed{(1, 5)}
\]

First, I need to determine which category this problem falls into. The user is asking for the slope of the line passing through two points (6, -4) and (-2, 8). This requires using the formula for calculating the slope between two points.

Next, applying the slope formula:

Slope (m) = (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁)

Let's identify the coordinates:
Point 1: (x₁, y₁) = (6, -4)
Point 2: (x₂, y₂) = (-2, 8)

Plugging in these values into the formula:

m = (8 - (-4)) / (-2 - 6)
= (12) / (-8)
= -1.5

Therefore, the slope of the line passing through the given points is -1.5.
</think>

To determine the category of this problem, let's analyze what it's asking for.

**Problem Statement:**  
"Find the slope of the line passing through the points (6, -4) and (-2, 8)."

### Solution:
The formula to find the **slope (m)** between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Given Points:**
- Point 1: \((6, -4)\)
- Point 2: \((-2, 8)\)

Plugging the values into the formula:

\[
m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{12}{-8} = -1.5
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The slope of the line passing through the points (6, -4) and (-2, 8) is **\(-1.5\)**.

tan(θ) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

In this case, θ is π/4, which has a tangent value of 1. So plugging that into the formula:

1 = |(m₂ - (1/4))/(1 + (1/4)*m₂)|

Since absolute value can give both positive and negative results, I need to consider two cases: one where (m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + m₂/4) equals 1 and another where it equals -1.

Solving for m₂:
Multiply both sides by denominator: m₂ - 1/4 = 1*(1 + m₂/4)
Expand the right side: m₂ - 1/4 = 1 + (m₂)/4
Bring all terms to left side: m₂ - (m₂)/4 - 1/4 -1 =0
Combine like terms: (3/4)m₂ -5/4=0
Add 5/4 to both sides: (3/4)m₂ =5/4
Multiply both sides by 4/3: m₂= (5/4)*(4/3)=5/3

Again, multiply both sides by denominator: m₂ -1/4 = -1*(1 + m₂/4)

Combine like terms: (5/4)m₂ +3/4=0

Calculate numerator: 5/3 -1/4 = (20/12 -3/12)=17/12
Denominator: 1 + 5/(12) =17/12
So tanθ= |(17/12)/(17/12)|=1, which is correct.

For m₂=-3/5:

Denominator: 1 -3/(20) =17/20

First, I need to identify the four given points that form a rectangle:
A(-2, 0), B(7, -3), C(2, 9), and D(-6, 7).

To calculate the area of the rectangle, I can use the distance formula to find the lengths of adjacent sides. I'll compute the distances between A and B, and between B and C.

</think>

To determine the area of the rectangle formed by the points \( A(-2, 0) \), \( B(7, -3) \), \( C(2, 9) \), and \( D(-6, 7) \), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the length of side AB:**
   
   Use the distance formula:
   \[
   AB = \sqrt{(x_B - x_A)^2 + (y_B - y_A)^2}
   \]
   Substituting the coordinates:
   \[
   AB = \sqrt{(7 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{9^2 + (-3)^2} = \sqrt{81 + 9} = \sqrt{90}
   \]

2. **Calculate the length of side BC:**
   
   Similarly, use the distance formula:
   \[
   BC = \sqrt{(x_C - x_B)^2 + (y_C - y_B)^2}
   \]
   Substituting the coordinates:
   \[
   BC = \sqrt{(2 - 7)^2 + (9 - (-3))^2} = \sqrt{(-5)^2 + 12^2} = \sqrt{25 + 144} = \sqrt{169} = 13
   \]

3. **Calculate the area of the rectangle:**
   
   Multiply the lengths of adjacent sides:
   \[
   \text{Area} = AB \times BC = \sqrt{90} \times 13
   \]
   Simplify \( \sqrt{90} \):
   \[
   \sqrt{90} = \sqrt{9 \times 10} = 3\sqrt{10}
   \]
   So,
   \[
   \text{Area} = 3\sqrt{10} \times 13 = 39\sqrt{10}
   \]

\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Since both distances from \((x, 0)\) to each of the points must be equal, I can set up the equation:
\[
\sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2}
\]

\[
(9 - x)^2 + 8^2 = (5 - x)^2 + 6^2
\]

To find a point on the **x-axis** that is **equidistant** from the points \((9,8)\) and \((5,6)\), follow these steps:

1. **Let the desired point be** \((x, 0)\).

2. **Set up the distance equations:**
   - Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((9, 8)\):
     \[
     d_1 = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2}
     \]
   - Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((5, 6)\):
     \[
     d_2 = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2}
     \]

3. **Since the distances are equal:**
   \[
   \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 8^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 6^2}
   \]
   
4. **Square both sides to eliminate the square roots:**
   \[
   (9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
   \]

5. **Expand and simplify the equation:**
   \[
   81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36
   \]
   
   \[
   145 - 18x = 61 - 10x
   \]

6. **Solve for \(x\):**
   \[
   -18x + 10x = 61 - 145
   \]
   
   \[
   -8x = -84
   \]
   
   \[
   x = \frac{-84}{-8} = 10.5
   \]

7. **Final Answer:**
   The point on the x-axis that is equidistant from \((9,8)\) and \((5,6)\) is:

Now, dividing the numerator by the denominator gives me:

\[ m = \frac{4}{8} \]

Simplifying this fraction, both the numerator and the denominator can be divided by 4. So, 4 ÷ 4 is 1, and 8 ÷ 4 is 2.

Therefore, the slope simplifies to \( \frac{1}{2} \) or 0.5 in decimal form.

\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{10 - 2} = \frac{4}{8} = \frac{1}{2} \]

slope₂ = (-1 - 5)/(-1 - 3) = (-6)/(-4) = 1.5 or 3/2

So, they are not negative reciprocals.

Checking Slope₁ (-1) with Slope₃ (1):
-1 * 1 = -1

Since the product is -1, these two slopes are negative reciprocals. This means that the lines connecting points (4, 4)-(3, 5) and (4, 4)-(-1, -1) are perpendicular to each other.

Therefore, the triangle formed by the three points has a right angle at point (4, 4).
</think>

To determine if the given points \((4, 4)\), \((3, 5)\), and \((-1, -1)\) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, we can analyze the slopes of the lines formed by these points.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Calculate the Slopes Between Each Pair of Points:**

   - **Slope Between \((4, 4)\) and \((3, 5)\):**
     \[
     m_1 = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = \frac{1}{-1} = -1
     \]
   
   - **Slope Between \((3, 5)\) and \((-1, -1)\):**
     \[
     m_2 = \frac{-1 - 5}{-1 - 3} = \frac{-6}{-4} = \frac{3}{2}
     \]
   
   - **Slope Between \((-1, -1)\) and \((4, 4)\):**
     \[
     m_3 = \frac{4 - (-1)}{4 - (-1)} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
     \]

2. **Check for Perpendicular Lines:**

   Two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes is \(-1\).

   - **Checking \(m_1\) and \(m_3\):**
     \[
     m_1 \times m_3 = (-1) \times 1 = -1
     \]
     Since the product is \(-1\), the lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_3\) are perpendicular.

### Conclusion:

The three points form a right-angled triangle at the point where the two perpendicular lines meet, which is \((4, 4)\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   - Given tan θ = 1/6, which represents the slope (m) of the line.
   - So, m = 1/6.

2. **Using X-intercept Form:**
   - The x-intercept is given as 6; thus, a = 6.
   - The intercept form equation is:
     \[
     \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
     \]
   - Plugging in a=6:
     \[
     \frac{x}{6} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
     \]

3. **Finding Y-intercept (b):**

     \[
     bx + 6y = 6b
     \]

     \[
     y = \left(-\frac{b}{6}\right)x + b
     \]

     \]
   - Solving for b:
     \[
     -b = 1 \\
     b = -1
     \]

     \[
     \frac{x}{6} + \frac{y}{-1} = 1 \\
     x/6 - y = 1
     \]
   - Rearranging to standard form:
     \[
     x - 6y = 6
     \]

First, I'll start by setting up the equation of the line in intercept form. Since the x-intercept and y-intercept are equal in magnitude but opposite in sign, let's denote the x-intercept as \(a\) and the y-intercept as \(-a\).

The general form of the line with these intercepts is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{-a} = 1
\]
Simplifying this equation gives:
\[
x - y = a
\]

To find the equation of the line that passes through the point \((4, 5)\) and has equal magnitude but opposite sign intercepts on the coordinate axes, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understanding the Intercepts
Let’s denote:
- **X-intercept** as \(a\)
- **Y-intercept** as \(-a\) (since they are equal in magnitude but opposite in sign)

The general equation of a line with intercepts on the x-axis and y-axis is given by:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
In our case, \(b = -a\). Substituting this into the equation gives:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{-a} = 1 \\
\Rightarrow x - y = a
\]

### Step 2: Using the Given Point to Find \(a\)
The line passes through the point \((4, 5)\). Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation:
\[
4 - 5 = a \\
\Rightarrow a = -1
\]

### Step 3: Writing the Final Equation of the Line
Substitute \(a = -1\) back into the simplified equation from Step 1:
\[
x - y = -1 \\
\Rightarrow x - y + 1 = 0
\]
Or, rearranged as:
\[
x - y = -1
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line is:

\boxed{x - y = -1}

To find the equation of the line passing through the points \((1, 0)\) and \((2, 3)\), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope**

The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the values from our points:
\[
m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3
\]

**Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation**

Point-slope form of a line's equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using one of the points, let's use \((1, 0)\):
\[
y - 0 = 3(x - 1) \\
y = 3x - 3
\]

**Final Equation in Slope-Intercept Form**
\[
y = 3x - 3
\]

Or, if you prefer the general form:
\[
3x - y - 3 = 0
\]

Calculating the numerator first: 8 - 7 equals 1. Then for the denominator: -5 - 4 is -9. So the slope m = 1 / (-9) which simplifies to -1/9.

Now that I have the slope, the next step is to use the point-slope form of a line's equation, which is y - y1 = m(x - x1). I can choose either of the two points for this; maybe (4,7) will be easier since 4 is positive and seems simpler.

Substituting into the formula: y - 7 = (-1/9)(x - 4).

Then, add 7 to both sides to isolate y: y = (-1/9)x + 4/9 + 7.

Simplify denominators: 8-7 is 1, and -5-4 is -9.

The equation of the line passing through points (4, 7) and (-5, 8) is derived as follows:

1. **Calculate the slope (m):**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]

2. **Use point-slope form with one of the points (e.g., (4, 7)):**
   \[
   y - 7 = -\frac{1}{9}(x - 4)
   \]
   
3. **Convert to slope-intercept form:**
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{9}x + \frac{4}{9} + 7
   \]
   Convert 7 to ninths:
   \[
   7 = \frac{63}{9}
   \]
   So,
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{9}x + \frac{4}{9} + \frac{63}{9} = -\frac{1}{9}x + \frac{67}{9}

Next, knowing that the general equation of a line in slope-intercept form is y = mx + b, and since it's at a distance of 5 units from the origin, we can use the formula for distance from a point to a line: |b| / sqrt(m² + 1) = 5.

To determine the equation of a line with an angle of \(-4^\circ\) relative to the x-axis and positioned at a perpendicular distance of 5 units on the left side from the origin, follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (m):**
   The slope \( m \) is given by:
   \[
   m = \tan(-4^\circ)
   \]
   Using a calculator:
   \[
   m \approx -0.0698
   \]

2. **Determine the Y-Intercept (b):**
   The general equation of a line in slope-intercept form is:
   \[
   y = mx + b
   \]
   The perpendicular distance \( d \) from the origin to the line is given by:
   \[
   d = \frac{|b|}{\sqrt{m^2 + 1}}
   \]
   Substituting \( d = 5 \) and solving for \( b \):
   \[
   5 = \frac{|b|}{\sqrt{(-0.0698)^2 + 1}} \\
   |b| = 5 \times \sqrt{(0.0698)^2 + 1} \\
   |b| \approx 5 \times 1.002437 \\
   |b| \approx 5.0122
   \]
   Since the line is on the left side (negative x-direction), we choose \( b = -5.0122 \).

3. **Final Equation of the Line:**
   Substituting \( m \) and \( b \) into the equation:
   \[
   y = -0.0698x - 5.0122
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{y = -0.0698x - 5.0122}
\]

I need to find the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line defined by the equation 5x - 6y -28 = 0.

First, I'll identify the coefficients and constants in the given line equation:
A = 5, B = -6, C = -28

Next, I'll apply the distance formula for a point (x₀, y₀) from the line Ax + By + C = 0:
Distance = |A*x₀ + B*y₀ + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)

Substituting the given values into the formula:
Numerator = |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) -28| = |25 + 42 -28| = |39| = 39

Denominator = sqrt(5² + (-6)^2) = sqrt(25 + 36) = sqrt(61)

Therefore, the distance is 39 divided by sqrt(61).

First, I need to determine the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\).

To find the distance between these lines, I can use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines. The general form of such lines is \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\), where \(A\) and \(B\) are coefficients that make the equations identical except for the constant term.

The formula to calculate the distance (\(d\)) between these two parallel lines is:

\[ d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Plugging in the values from the given equations, where \(C_1 = 12\) and \(C_2 = 7\), I can compute the numerator as the absolute difference between these constants.

To find the **distance** between the two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[
3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad 3x - 4y + 7 = 0
\]

we can use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines. The general form of such lines is \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\), where \(A\) and \(B\) are coefficients that make the equations identical except for the constant term.

The formula to calculate the distance (\(d\)) between these two parallel lines is:

\[
d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients \(A\), \(B\), \(C_1\), and \(C_2\):**
   
   From the given equations:
   \[
   3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \quad (C_1 = 12)
   \]
   \[
   3x - 4y + 7 = 0 \quad (C_2 = 7)
   \]
   
   Here, \(A = 3\), \(B = -4\).

2. **Calculate the numerator:**
   
   \[
   |C_1 - C_2| = |12 - 7| = |5| = 5
   \]

3. **Calculate the denominator:**
   
   \[
   \sqrt{A^2 + B^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

4. **Compute the distance \(d\):**
   
   \[
   d = \frac{5}{5} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: 1}
\]

First, I need to understand what the user is asking for. They provided two equations of lines and asked for the angle between them.

I'll rewrite it in slope-intercept form (y = mx + b) to identify its slope.
Subtracting sqrt(3)x from both sides gives y = -sqrt(3)x + 1. So, the slope m₁ is -sqrt(3).

The second line is x + sqrt(3)y = 1.
I'll also rewrite this in slope-intercept form. Subtracting x from both sides and dividing by sqrt(3) gives y = (-1/sqrt(3))x + 1/sqrt(3). So, the slope m₂ is -1/sqrt(3).

Next, I'll use the formula for the angle between two lines:
tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Plugging in the values:
tanθ = |[(-1/sqrt(3)) - (-sqrt(3))] / [1 + (-sqrt(3))*(-1/sqrt(3))]|
= |[(-1/sqrt(3) + sqrt(3))] / [1 + 1]|
= |[(sqrt(3) - 1/sqrt(3))] / 2|

To simplify, I'll rationalize the numerator:
Multiply numerator and denominator by sqrt(3):
= |[( (sqrt(3))^2 - 1 ) / sqrt(3)] / 2|
= |[ (3 - 1)/sqrt(3) ] / 2|
= |[2/sqrt(3)] / 2|
= |1/sqrt(3)|

Taking the arctangent to find θ:
θ = arctan(1/sqrt(3)) = 30 degrees.

Finally, I'll convert this angle into radians for a more precise answer.
Since π radians equal 180 degrees,
θ ≈ (π/6) radians ≈ 0.5236 radians.

So, the angle between the two lines is approximately 0.524 radians.

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1
\]
and
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slopes of the Lines

First, rewrite each equation in slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \), where \( m \) is the slope.

**Line 1:**  
\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \\
y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
\]
So, the slope (\( m_1 \)) is:
\[
m_1 = -\sqrt{3}
\]

**Line 2:**  
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \\
\sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
y = \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
So, the slope (\( m_2 \)) is:
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

### Step 2: Use the Formula for the Angle Between Two Lines

The formula to find the angle \( \theta \) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

Plugging in the values:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3}) \cdot \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)} \right|
\]

Simplify the numerator and denominator:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3}}{1 + 1} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right|
\]

Rationalize the numerator:
\[
\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{3 - 1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

So,
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right|
\]

### Step 3: Find the Angle \( \theta \)

Take the arctangent of both sides:
\[
\theta = \arctan\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right) \\
= 30^\circ
\]

Convert degrees to radians (since \( 180^\circ = \pi \) radians):
\[
\theta = \frac{\pi}{6} \approx 0.524 \text{ radians}
\]

### Final Answer

The angle between the two lines is:

{"Final Answer": "0.524"}
```

To find the equation of a line parallel to \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) and passing through the point where \( x = 5 \), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**
   The given line is in standard form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \). To find its slope:
   \[
   y = \frac{A}{B}x - \frac{C}{B}
   \]
   Here, \( A = 1 \), \( B = -7 \), and \( C = 5 \). Thus,
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7}
   \]
   The slope (\( m \)) of the given line is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

2. **Equation of Parallel Line:**
   Since parallel lines have the same slope, the desired line also has a slope of \( \frac{1}{7} \).

3. **Using Point-Slope Form:**
   The point where \( x = 5 \) implies the point is \( (5, y) \). However, since only the value of \( x \) is given and no corresponding \( y \), we can assume it passes through \( (5, 0) \) for simplicity.

4. **Constructing the Equation:**
   Using the slope-intercept form:
   \[
   y = mx + b \\
   y = \frac{1}{7}x + b
   \]
   Plugging in \( x = 5 \) and \( y = 0 \):
   \[
   0 = \frac{1}{7}(5) + b \\
   b = -\frac{5}{7}
   \]
   
5. **Final Equation:**
   Substituting back:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{7}x - \frac{5}{7}
   \]
   Multiplying both sides by 7 to eliminate fractions:
   \[
   7y = x - 5 \\
   x - 7y - 5 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x - 7y - 5 = 0}

To find the distance between the two parallel lines \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) and \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Verify Parallelism
First, ensure that both equations represent parallel lines by simplifying the second equation.

Simplify the second line:
\[
38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 19x + 12y + 35 = 0
\]
This matches the form of the first equation \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\), confirming that they are parallel.

### Step 2: Apply the Distance Formula for Parallel Lines
The distance between two parallel lines \(ax + by + c_1 = 0\) and \(ax + by + c_2 = 0\) is given by:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]
Here, \(a = 19\), \(b = 12\), \(c_1 = -38\), and \(c_2 = 35\).

### Step 3: Calculate the Numerator
Compute the difference between the constants:
\[
|c_1 - c_2| = |-38 - 35| = |-73| = 73
\]

### Step 4: Calculate the Denominator
Compute the square root of the sum of squares of \(a\) and \(b\):
\[
\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2} = \sqrt{361 + 144} = \sqrt{505}
\]

### Step 5: Compute the Distance
Divide the numerator by the denominator:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
\]
This can be rationalized as:
\[
\frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
\]

To find the distance from the point (1, 2) to the line \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\), we use the formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the given values:
- \(A = 4\), \(B = 7\), \(C = 5\)
- \(x_0 = 1\), \(y_0 = 2\)

Calculate the numerator:
\[
|4 \times 1 + 7 \times 2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23
\]

Calculate the denominator:
\[
\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}
\]

Thus, the distance is:
\[
\frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

Final Answer:  
The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\) is \(\frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}\).

   - Length between (-6,7) and (2,9):
     \[

**Solution:**

To determine if the triangle formed by the points \( (4, 4) \), \( (3, 5) \), and \( (-1, -1) \) has a right angle without using the Pythagorean theorem, we can check if any two sides are perpendicular. Two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes equals \(-1\).

1. **Calculate the Slopes Between Each Pair of Points:**

   - **Slope between \( (4, 4) \) and \( (3, 5) \):**
     \[
     m_1 = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = \frac{1}{-1} = -1
     \]
   
   - **Slope between \( (4, 4) \) and \( (-1, -1) \):**
     \[
     m_2 = \frac{-1 - 4}{-1 - 4} = \frac{-5}{-5} = 1
     \]
   
   - **Slope between \( (3, 5) \) and \( (-1, -1) \):**
     \[
     m_3 = \frac{-1 - 5}{-1 - 3} = \frac{-6}{-4} = \frac{3}{2}
     \]

2. **Check for Perpendicularity:**

   For any two sides to be perpendicular, the product of their slopes must be \(-1\).

   - \( m_1 \times m_2 = (-1) \times (1) = -1 \)
   
   Since this condition is met between \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \), the sides with these slopes are perpendicular, indicating a right angle.

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{\text{The triangle has a right angle.}}

Given two lines intersecting at an angle whose tangent is \( \frac{1}{3} \), we need to find the possible slopes of one line given that another line with slope \( \frac{2}{m} \) intersects it.

First, we use the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \):
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
Given that \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \), we let one line have slope \( m \) and the other line have slope \( \frac{2}{m} \). Substituting these slopes into the formula, we get:
\[
\left| \frac{\frac{2}{m} - m}{1 + m \cdot \frac{2}{m}} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Simplifying the denominator:
\[
1 + 2 = 3
\]
Thus, the equation becomes:
\[
\left| \frac{\frac{2}{m} - m}{3} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Multiplying both sides by 3:
\[
\left| \frac{2}{m} - m \right| = 1
\]
This results in two cases:
1. \( \frac{2}{m} - m = 1 \)
2. \( -\left( \frac{2}{m} - m \right) = 1 \)

For the first case:
\[
\frac{2}{m} - m = 1
\]
Multiplying by \( m \):
\[
2 - m^2 = m
\]
Rearranging:
\[
m^2 + m - 2 = 0
\]
Solving this quadratic equation:
\[
m = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 8}}{2} = \frac{-1 \pm 3}{2}
\]
This gives solutions \( m = 1 \) and \( m = -2 \).

For the second case:
\[
-\left( \frac{2}{m} - m \right) = 1
\]
Simplifying:
\[
-m + \frac{2}{m} = 1
\]
Multiplying by \( m \):
\[
-2 + m^2 = m
\]
Rearranging:
\[
m^2 - m - 2 = 0
\]
Solving this quadratic equation:
\[
m = \frac{1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 8}}{2} = \frac{1 \pm 3}{2}
\]
This gives solutions \( m = 2 \) and \( m = -1 \).

Thus, the possible slopes for the first line are \( -2, -1, 1, 2 \).

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{-2}, \boxed{-1}, \boxed{1}, \boxed{2}
\]

To find the equation of the line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope**

The formula for the slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

**Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form**

The point-slope form of a line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]

Using the point (3, 1):
\[
y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
\]

**Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form**

Solve for \( y \) to get the equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)):
\[
y - 1 = 3x - 9
\]
\[
y = 3x - 8
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}
\]

- Let \( p \) represent the price per liter (in Rs).
- Let \( q \) represent the quantity sold (in liters).

We can set up two equations based on the given data points:

\[
q = m \cdot p + b
\]

Where:
- \( m \) is the slope of the line.
- \( b \) is the y-intercept.

Using the first data point (\( p_1, q_1 \)):

\[
1080 = m \cdot 114 + b
\]

And the second data point (\( p_2, q_2 \)):

\[
1320 = m \cdot 132 + b
\]

Now, we can solve for \( m \) by subtracting the first equation from the second:

\[
1320 - 1080 = (m \cdot 132 + b) - (m \cdot 114 + b)
\]
\[
240 = m \cdot 18
\]
\[
m = \frac{240}{18} = \frac{40}{3} \approx 13.333...
\]

With \( m \) known, we can substitute it back into one of the original equations to solve for \( b \). Using the first equation:

\[

\]
\[
1080 = 1520 + b
\]
\[

\]

Now, we have the linear equation:

\[
q = \frac{40}{3}p - 440
\]

Finally, to find \( q \) when \( p = 117 \):

\[
q = \frac{40}{3}(117) - 440
\]
\[
q = 1560 - 440 = 1120
\]

Therefore, at a price of 117 Rs per liter, the quantity sold would be **1120 liters**.

To determine the equation of a line equidistant from the point (2, 13), we'll follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Slope**:
   - Since no specific slope is given, let's assume an arbitrary slope for simplicity. Let's choose \( m = 2 \).

2. **Write the General Equation of the Line**:
   - The general form with the assumed slope is:
     \[
     y = 2x + b
     \]
   
3. **Calculate the Distance from the Point to the Line**:
   - The distance (\(d\)) from a point \( (x_0, y_0) \) to the line \( ax + by + c = 0 \) is given by:
     \[
     d = \frac{|ax_0 + by_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
     \]
   - For our assumed slope, rewrite the equation in standard form:
     \[
     2x - y + b = 0
     \]
   - Now, plug in the point (2, 13):
     \[
     d = \frac{|2(2) - 1(13) + b|}{\sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{|4 - 13 + b|}{\sqrt{5}} = \frac{|b - 9|}{\sqrt{5}}
     \]
   
4. **Set Up Equations for Equal Distances**:
   - Since the line is equidistant from (2,13), we have two possible scenarios with opposite signs:
     \[
     b - 9 = d \quad \text{and} \quad -(b) + (-d) = ?
     \]

The equation of the line passing through the points A(2, 3) and B(4, 5) can be found using the slope-intercept form. First, calculate the slope \( m \):

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{5 - 3}{4 - 2} = \frac{2}{2} = 1
\]

Using point A(2, 3) and the slope \( m = 1 \), apply the point-slope formula:

\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
\[
y - 3 = 1(x - 2)
\]
\[
y = x - 2 + 3
\]
\[
y = x + 1
\]

So, the equation of the line is \( y = x + 1 \).

First, calculate the slope \( m \):
\[

\]

\[ d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Rewriting the equation in standard form:
\[ 4x + y - b = 0 \]
Here, \( A = 4 \), \( B = 1 \), and \( C = -b \).

The point given is (-4, y). However, since only the X-coordinate is specified, I assume that at this X value, the distance from the line to the X-axis (where Y=0) is 5 units. This implies:
\[ |4(-4) + 1(0) - b| = 5 \sqrt{4^2 + 1^2} \]
Simplifying:
\[ |-16 - b| = 5 \times \sqrt{17} \]

This leads to two possible equations for \( b \):
1. \( -16 - b = 5\sqrt{17} \) which gives \( b = -16 - 5\sqrt{17} \)
2. \( -16 - b = -5\sqrt{17} \) which gives \( b = -16 + 5\sqrt{17} \)

Therefore, there are two possible equations for the line:
\[ y = -4x - 16 - 5\sqrt{17} \]
and
\[ y = -4x - 16 + 5\sqrt{17} \]

These represent the lines with a slope of -4 that are 5 units away from the X-axis at \( x = -4 \).

   \[
   y = -4x + b
   \]
   
2. **Distance Formula**: The distance (\( d \)) from the X-axis (where \( y = 0 \)) at a specific point is given by:
   \[
   |b| = 5\sqrt{1 + (-4)^2} = 5\sqrt{17}
   \]
   
3. **Solving for \( b \)**: Therefore, the Y-intercept (\( b \)) can be either positive or negative:
   \[
   b = \pm 5\sqrt{17}
   \]
   
4. **Final Equations**: The equations of the lines are:
   \[
   y = -4x + 5\sqrt{17} \quad \text{and} \quad y = -4x - 5\sqrt{17}
   \]

To find the **distance from the point (5, -7)** to the line defined by the equation \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we'll use the formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Given:**
- Line equation: \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\) (so, \(A = 5\), \(B = -6\), \(C = -28\))
- Point: \((x_0, y_0) = (5, -7)\)

**Solution:**

1. **Calculate the numerator:**
   \[
   |A x_0 + B y_0 + C| = |5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|
   \]
   \[
   = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]

2. **Calculate the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{A^2 + B^2} = \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}
   \]
   \[
   = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

3. **Compute the distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]

I need to find the distance between two parallel lines given by their equations.

First, I'll rewrite both equations in slope-intercept form to confirm they are indeed parallel. 

For the first equation:
3x - 4y + 12 = 0  
Subtracting 3x and adding 4y:  
-4y = -3x + 12  
Dividing by -4:  
y = (3/4)x - 3

For the second equation:
3x - 4y + 7 = 0  
Following similar steps:  
-4y = -3x + 7  
Dividing by -4:  
y = (3/4)x - (7/4)

Both lines have the same slope of 3/4, confirming they are parallel.

Next, I'll use the distance formula for parallel lines. The formula is |c1 - c2| divided by the square root of (A squared plus B squared), where A and B are coefficients from the line equation Ax + By + C = 0.

Rewriting both equations in the form Ax + By + C = 0:
First line: 3x - 4y + 12 = 0  
Second line: 3x - 4y + 7 = 0

Here, A = 3, B = -4 for both lines.

Calculating the absolute difference in the constants: |12 - 7| = 5

Now, compute the denominator by finding the square root of (A² + B²):  
√(3² + (-4)²) = √(9 + 16) = √25 = 5

Finally, divide the absolute difference by the denominator to find the distance:  
Distance = |12 - 7| / 5 = 5/5 = 1
</think>

To determine the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[
3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad 3x - 4y + 7 = 0
\]

we can use the formula for the distance \( d \) between two parallel lines \( Ax + By + C_1 = 0 \) and \( Ax + By + C_2 = 0 \):

\[
d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Coefficients:**
   - For both lines, \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \).
   - Constants:
     - \( C_1 = 12 \)
     - \( C_2 = 7 \)

2. **Apply the Distance Formula:**
   \[
   d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
   \]

tanθ = |(m2 - m1) / (1 + m1*m2)|

tanθ = |(-1/√3 - (-√3)) / (1 + (-√3)*(-1/√3))|

Simplify numerator and denominator:
Numerator: -1/√3 + √3 = (√3)/√3 - 1/√3 = (√3 - 1)/√3
Denominator: 1 + (√3)*(1/√3) = 1 + 1 = 2

So, tanθ = |(√3 - 1)/(√3 * 2)|

Simplify further:
tanθ = |(√3 - 1)/(2√3)|

Multiply numerator and denominator by √3 to rationalize:
tanθ = |( (√3)(√3) - √3 ) / (2*3)| = |(3 - √3)/6|

Now, calculate the angle θ using arctangent:
θ = arctan(|(3 - √3)/6|)

Using a calculator, find that θ is approximately 15 degrees.
</think>

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1
\]
and
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

**Step 1: Find the slopes of both lines.**

For the first equation:
\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \\
y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
\]
So, the slope (\(m_1\)) is \(-\sqrt{3}\).

For the second equation:
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \\
\sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
y = \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
So, the slope (\(m_2\)) is \(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\).

**Step 2: Use the formula to find the angle between two lines.**

The formula for the tangent of the angle (\(\theta\)) between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is:
\[
\tan{\theta} = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

**Step 3: Plug in the values of \(m_1\) and \(m_2\).**

\[
\tan{\theta} = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + \left(-\sqrt{3}\right) \cdot \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)} \right|
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the expression.**

\[
\tan{\theta} = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3}}{1 + 1} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\frac{3 - 1}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{2/\sqrt{3}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right|
\]

**Step 5: Find the angle using the arctangent function.**

\[
\theta = \arctan\left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right) \\
\theta = 30^\circ
\]

**Final Answer:**

{"Final Answer": "30°"}
```

To determine the distance between two parallel lines, I first need to confirm that they are indeed parallel. This can be done by checking if their slopes are equal.

The given equations of the lines are:
19x + 12y - 38 = 0 and 38x + 24y + 70 = 0

Simplifying the second equation, I notice that it's exactly twice the first equation. This indicates that both lines have the same slope, confirming they are parallel.

Next, I will use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines Ax + By + C1 = 0 and Ax + By + C2 = 0:

Distance = |C1 - C2| / sqrt(A² + B²)

Applying this formula to the given equations:
A = 19, B = 12
For the first line: C1 = -38
For the second line: After simplifying, C2 = -70

Calculating the distance:
Distance = |(-38) - (-70)| / sqrt(19² + 12²)
= |32| / sqrt(461)
≈ 32 / 21.47 ≈ 1.49 units

To find the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[
19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad 38x + 24y + 70 = 0
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify Parallelism:**
   - First, check if the lines are parallel by comparing their coefficients.
   - Notice that the second equation is exactly twice the first equation:
     \[
     38x + 24y = 76 \quad (\text{twice } 19x + 12y = 38)
     \]
   - Since both equations are multiples of each other, the lines are **parallel**.

2. **Simplify the Second Equation:**
   - Divide the second equation by 2 to make it comparable with the first:
     \[
     19x + 12y + 35 = 0
     \]
   
3. **Use the Distance Formula Between Parallel Lines:**
   - The general formula for the distance \( D \) between two parallel lines \( Ax + By + C_1 = 0 \) and \( Ax + By + C_2 = 0 \) is:
     \[
     D = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
     \]
   - Here, \( A = 19 \), \( B = 12 \), \( C_1 = -38 \), and \( C_2 = 35 \).

4. **Calculate the Distance:**
   \[
   D = \frac{|-38 - 35|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \approx 5.19 \text{ units}
   \]

To find the distance between the two parallel lines \(3x + 4y = 9\) and \(6x + 8y = 18\), follow these steps:

First, check if the lines are indeed parallel by comparing their slopes.

- **Line 1**: \(3x + 4y = 9\)

Plugging these into the formula, it should be |4*4 + (-1)*1 + 0| divided by sqrt(4² + (-1)²). Let me compute that step by step.

First, calculate the numerator: 4*4 = 16, then -1*1 = -1. Adding them together with c which is 0 gives 16 -1 + 0 = 15. The absolute value of 15 is still 15.

Now for the denominator: sqrt(4² + (-1)²) = sqrt(16 + 1) = sqrt(17).

So the distance should be 15 divided by sqrt(17). But wait, usually we rationalize the denominator. So multiplying numerator and denominator by sqrt(17), I get (15*sqrt(17)) / 17.

Let me double-check if I applied the formula correctly. The line was given as 4x - y = 0, so yes, a=4, b=-1, c=0. Point is (4,1). Plugging into |ax₀ + by₀ + c| gives |16 -1 +0|=15. Denominator sqrt(16+1)=sqrt(17). So the calculation seems correct.

I also remember that distance should always be positive, and this result is positive, so that makes sense. I don't see any mistakes in my steps.
</think>

The distance from point P(4, 1) to the line 4x - y = 0 can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

Distance = \(\frac{|4*4 - 1|}{\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}} = \frac{15\sqrt{17}}{17}\)

I need to find the equation of a line that is parallel to both 9x + 6y -7 =0 and 3x +2y +6=0. Since these two lines are parallel, they must have the same slope.

First, I'll convert both equations into slope-intercept form (y = mx + b) to find their slopes and y-intercepts.

For the first equation:
9x +6y -7=0
Subtract 9x and add 7 to both sides: 6y = -9x +7
Divide by 6: y = (-9/6)x + 7/6 → y = -1.5x + 1.1667

For the second equation:
3x +2y +6=0
Subtract 3x and subtract 6 from both sides: 2y = -3x -6
Divide by 2: y = (-3/2)x -3 → y = -1.5x -3

Both lines have the same slope of -1.5, confirming they are parallel.

Next, I'll find a point (a, b) that lies on both original lines to determine where the new line will pass through.

Setting the two equations equal since they intersect at this common point:
-1.5a + 1.1667 = -1.5a -3
This simplifies to 1.1667 = -3, which is impossible. This indicates that there's no single point where both lines meet; instead, they are parallel and do not intersect.

Since the new line must be parallel to both given lines, it will have the same slope of -1.5 (or 3/2 when expressed as a positive numerator). Therefore, its equation will be y = (3/2)x + b, where b is the y-intercept we need to determine.

To find the equation of a line that is parallel to both \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) and \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\), follow these steps:

1. **Convert Both Equations to Slope-Intercept Form:**

   For the first equation:
   \[
   9x + 6y - 7 = 0 \\
   6y = -9x + 7 \\
   y = -\frac{3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6}
   \]
   
   For the second equation:
   \[
   3x + 2y + 6 = 0 \\
   2y = -3x - 6 \\
   y = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3
   \]

2. **Determine the Slope:**

   Both lines have the same slope of \(-\frac{3}{2}\), confirming they are parallel.

3. **Find a Point Common to Both Lines (If Any):**

   Set the two equations equal to find if there's an intersection point:
   \[
   -\frac{3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6} = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3
   \]
   
   Subtracting \(-\frac{3}{2}x\) from both sides results in:
   \[
   \frac{7}{6} = -3
   \]
   
   This is a contradiction, indicating there's no single point where both lines meet. Therefore, the only way to define a new line parallel to both is by specifying its slope and y-intercept.

4. **Define the New Line:**

   Since the new line must be parallel, it will have the same slope of \(-\frac{3}{2}\). The equation can be expressed as:
   \[
   y = -\frac{3}{2}x + b
   \]
   
   To find \(b\), additional information is needed (e.g., a point through which the line passes).

5. **Final Equation:**

   Without additional information, we cannot determine the exact value of \(b\). However, knowing that it has the same slope as both original lines allows us to express it in terms of \(b\).

**Answer:** {'Final Answer': 'The equation of the parallel line is y = -1.5x + b where b is not provided without additional information.'}
